{"result":"{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_1/projects/struct_self_consist_ie/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — struct_self_consist_ie\\n\\n> Updated 2026-05-15 — D098: 4B scale ablation 2×2 矩阵完成。4B ≈ 8B（多数 correlation 4B 反而更高），gap 与 model capacity 无关，task difficulty 是信号退化主驱动。Scale ablation 证明 universality across scales。R14 RESOLVED。\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: EMNLP 2026 Findings (via ARR May 2026)\\n- Deadline: ARR May cycle, 2026-05-25\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条, D072 updated — ablation proves universality)\\n\\n### Claim 1 (PRIMARY — Universal Correlation-Selection Gap)\\nThe correlation-selection gap in structured IE is a **fundamental property of the task**, not an artifact of constrained decoding. Confidence signals reliably predict output quality (AUROC up to 0.75) but cannot select better candidates from multiple samples. A controlled free-form decoding ablation proves this gap is universal: removing constrained decoding does NOT recover logprob variance or selection ability.\\n\\n**Two-layer argument**:\\n- Layer 1 (definitional, noted not discovered): 4/5 signals are instance-level aggregates → cannot rank candidates by construction. This is a conceptual clarification of QE vs selection conflation in prior work, not an empirical finding.\\n- Layer 2 (empirical, the core contribution): (a) Logprob — the only per-sample signal — fails due to intrinsic score compression (60% of instances have within-sample range < 0.05). (b) Consensus-proximity/MBR strategies also fail despite success in MT (non-trivial). (c) **Free-form ablation FALSIFIES regime-specific hypothesis**: removing constrained decoding does NOT recover logprob discrimination (std ratio 0.97, tied fraction -2pp) → compression is model/task intrinsic, proving universality via elimination.\\n\\n**Key evidence (score compression)**:\\n- Non-tied subset (40%): logprob selection +2.9pp vs greedy (p=0.01), within-instance ρ: 0.08→0.39\\n- Full set: logprob +0.6pp, bootstrap p=0.39, Cohen's d=0.038 (negligible), 95% CI [-3.0, +4.2]pp\\n- Oracle headroom: NER +13pp, RE +18pp → information exists but signals can't access it\\n\\n**Key evidence (free-form ablation)** [DONE — exp_freeform_ablation, NEGATIVE]:\\n- LP distribution: constrained std=0.0206, free-form std=0.0199 (ratio 0.97, virtually unchanged)\\n- Tied fraction: 59.7% → 57.7% (-2.0pp, negligible)\\n- NER selection: +0.59pp → +0.18pp (both negligible vs greedy)\\n- RE selection: -0.68pp → +1.69pp (flipped positive — secondary finding)\\n- NER VC rho: 0.379 → 0.409 (slight QE improvement, selection still fails)\\n- Parse rate: 99.95% (fine-tuned model retains JSON format without grammar)\\n- **Conclusion**: Score compression is model/task intrinsic. The gap is universal.\\n\\n**Key evidence (scale ablation)** [DONE — 2×2 matrix: {CoNLL, SciERC} × {Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-4B}]:\\n- CoNLL NER: 4B greedy_f1=0.9234 (>8B 0.9123, +1.11pp), SJ_ρ=0.4217 (≈8B 0.4357, −1.4pp), FK_ρ=0.4181 (>8B 0.3998, +1.83pp)\\n- SciERC NER: 4B SJ_ρ=0.4221 (>8B 0.3825, +3.96pp), FK_ρ=0.3258 (>8B 0.2590, +6.68pp)\\n- SciERC RE: 4B SJ_ρ=0.2673 (>8B 0.2441, +2.32pp), FK_ρ=0.3593 (>8B 0.2782, +8.11pp); struct>surface ✗ at both scales\\n- **Conclusion**: Correlation strength maintained or improved at 4B across all 4 cells. The gap originates from within-instance signal misalignment, not model capacity. Task difficulty (SciERC RE zero-F1 rate) remains the primary driver, consistent across scales.\\n- CoNLL 4B headroom: 1.70pp (vs 8B 4.20pp) → MARGINAL for selection power, but QE correlations robust\\n- SciERC RE 4B: 47.6% zero-F1, RE LP_ρ_cond=0.1135 (p=0.074, n.s.) → confirms binary dominance at 4B\\n- **4B selection results** (correlation-selection gap at 4B):\\n  - CoNLL NER 4B (n=2756): SJ +0.07pp, FK +0.03pp, LP +0.09pp vs greedy 0.9213. All ≤0.1pp — MARGINAL (headroom 1.70pp)\\n  - SciERC NER 4B (n=529): SJ **-1.49pp**, FK -1.56pp, LP -2.23pp vs greedy 0.6622. Oracle 0.7925 (+13.02pp). **All signals WORSE than greedy despite 13pp oracle headroom — gap confirmed at 4B**\\n  - SciERC NER 4B gap demonstration: SJ-FK ρ gap = +0.096 (strong correlation advantage), SJ-FK selection F1 gap = +0.0007 (zero selection advantage) → correlation-selection gap quantified at 4B\\n- **n_samples confound control**: Qwen3-8B n=8 data exists as control. CoNLL SJ: n8 ρ=0.4389 → n16 ρ=0.4496 (Δ=0.011, CI crosses zero). SciERC SJ: n8→n16 Δ=0.066, CI=[0.004, 0.128] weak effect. n_samples not a confound for 4B-8B comparison.\\n- **Paper framing**: \\\"Scale ablation shows the gap is not an artifact of a particular model size; correlation strength is maintained at 4B, confirming that the gap originates from within-instance signal misalignment rather than model capacity.\\\"\\n\\n**exp-019 supervised verifier**: SKIPPED (Reviewer recommendation). Reasons: DDL 10 days, scope creep risk (analytical paper + solution = type mismatch), exp-016 LR ensemble already underperforms EM (0.666 vs 0.749). Verifier is follow-up paper material.\\n\\n**Bonus finding (parse rate)**: Free-form parse rate 99.95% (4406/4408) — fine-tuned 8B model generates valid JSON without grammar constraints. Challenges intro:19 claim about constrained decoding necessity for format compliance.\\n\\n**Implications**: Not just \\\"signals don't work\\\" but \\\"here's WHY they don't work (intrinsic score compression), here's proof it's universal (controlled ablation via elimination), and here's the forward direction (breaking LP compression requires fundamentally different approaches — trained verifiers, multi-round refinement — not simply relaxing decoding constraints).\\\"\\n\\n### Claim 2 (METHODOLOGY — QE Evaluation Framework)\\nSystematic evaluation of 5 confidence signals across 4 families for structured IE quality estimation. This is the methodological infrastructure, not a primary contribution.\\n\\n**Key results**:\\n- NER: EM AUROC 0.749, voting_conf ρ=0.379, SJ ρ=0.360 — all >> logprob (AUROC 0.578, ρ=0.205). 3-seed consistent: SJ > FK delta=0.093~0.122\\n- RE (3-seed confirmed): **LP > SJ > FK** (mean ρ: LP 0.302, SJ 0.265, FK 0.254). Signal ranking reverses vs NER — structural signals lose advantage when 43-45% of RE samples have F1=0 (binary signal dominance). LP's coarse but stable generation-confidence signal captures \\\"did the model get any relations right\\\" better than structural overlap measures.\\n- Difficulty-dependent signal ranking: WNUT-17 conditional collapse (ρ significant → n.s.) + logprob ρ=−0.410 (non-obvious: overconfidence on OOD)\\n- Temperature robustness: SJ Δρ=±0.01 vs FK Δρ=±0.06-0.09. T=0.7: LP full ρ improves 0.205→0.249, LP cond ρ 0.154→0.185; FK 0.267→0.289 despite more compression → concentrated signal hypothesis (non-tied instances at lower T carry more informative variance). **Paper citation rule**: robustness/temperature tables use full ρ; score compression analysis uses cond ρ. Always annotate which variant.\\n- **Task-dependent signal effectiveness** (new finding from RE 3-seed): The optimal signal family depends on task structure. NER (token-level, graded F1) → structural agreement captures quality gradients. RE (relation-level, binary-dominated F1) → logprob captures generation confidence better. This is NOT a weakness — it's a nuanced finding that enriches the diagnostic framework.\\n\\n**Evidence preserved from original Claim 1** (SciERC, N=8, T=1.0, exp_012_rerun_1024):\\n- NER full (n=529): ρ — voting_conf 0.379, SJ 0.360, EM 0.295, FK 0.267, logprob 0.205; AUROC — EM 0.749, SJ 0.692, voting_conf 0.684, FK 0.629, logprob 0.578\\n- RE full (n=397): AUROC — EM 0.706, voting_conf 0.694, SJ 0.670, FK 0.609, logprob 0.601\\n- ECE (NER): EM 0.353, voting 0.381, SJ 0.453, FK 0.451, logprob 0.706\\n\\n### Claim 2b (Selection evidence)\\n**exp_016_rerun_1024 evidence** (SciERC 8B, N=8, T=1.0):\\n- NER best-of-N (n=529): logprob 0.6501 > sj 0.6450 = ensemble 0.6450 > greedy 0.6439 > voting 0.6337 > random 0.6077 (oracle 0.7758)\\n- RE best-of-N (n=397): greedy 0.4042 > logprob 0.3948 > sj 0.3855 = ensemble 0.3855 > voting 0.3592 > random 0.3544 (oracle 0.5850)\\n\\n**4B selection evidence** (N=8, T=1.0):\\n- CoNLL NER 4B (n=2756): greedy 0.9213, SJ 0.9220 (+0.07pp), FK 0.9215, LP 0.9221 (+0.09pp), oracle 0.9382. Headroom 1.70pp → selection indistinguishable\\n- SciERC NER 4B (n=529): greedy 0.6622, SJ 0.6473 (-1.49pp), FK 0.6466 (-1.56pp), LP 0.6399 (-2.23pp), oracle 0.7925 (+13.02pp). **All signals worse than greedy** → correlation-selection gap at 4B confirmed\\n\\n**exp_016_rerun_1024_bootstrap evidence** (3-seed bootstrap, 1000 iterations):\\n- NER logprob vs greedy: bootstrap 95% CI [−0.030, +0.042], permutation p=0.39\\n- Effect size: Cohen's d=0.038 (negligible), sign test win rate=0.466\\n\\n### Claim 3 (Diagnostic — task-complexity boundary + task-dependent signal effectiveness)\\nThe effectiveness of consistency-based confidence is governed by task structural complexity: compound error propagation in relational tasks degrades the consistency-accuracy correlation on the full instance set, but the signal remains valid within the model's capability range. This boundary applies across all confidence signals tested.\\n\\n**RE 3-seed mechanism** (D073, confirmed at 4B): 43-47% of RE samples have F1=0 (8B: 43-45%, 4B: 47.6%), creating binary-dominated signal distributions where structural overlap (SJ/FK) cannot differentiate quality gradients. Pattern consistent across model scales — confirming task difficulty, not model capacity, drives this boundary. LP's generation confidence captures \\\"complete failure vs partial success\\\" more reliably. 3-seed RE QE: LP ρ=0.302 > SJ 0.265 > FK 0.254. **Definitive binary dominance evidence**: RE LP full ρ=0.266 but cond ρ=0.011 (p=0.86, completely n.s.) — LP's entire RE signal comes from the zero/nonzero F1 split; once conditioned on samples where the model extracts at least one relation, LP has zero predictive power for RE quality gradients. In contrast, NER LP cond ρ remains informative (0.154–0.185). This provides the concrete mechanism for Claim 3's task-complexity boundary: it's not just \\\"compound error\\\" in the abstract — it's specifically the high zero-F1 rate that shifts the optimal signal family from structural to probabilistic, and LP's RE advantage is entirely a binary classification effect, not genuine quality estimation.\\n\\n**MVP evidence**: RE full vs cond AUROC lift — EM: 0.706→0.752 (+4.6pp), SJ: 0.670→0.646 (−2.4pp, 待确认), voting_conf: 0.694→0.642 (−5.2pp, 待确认). Component decomposition shows all RE components equally weak (head_sj rho=0.210). T=0.7 naturally separates knowledge-gap vs uncertainty errors.\\n\\n**exp-005 evidence**: Conditional filtering dwarfs temperature tuning — RE SJ cond lift +0.07~0.14 vs temperature effect ±0.01. T=1.0 preserves more oracle headroom (RE: 0.167 vs 0.131) and diversity (1-SJ: 0.389 vs 0.313). Recommendation: T=1.0 + conditional filtering, not temperature optimization.\\n\\n**exp-001 N-scaling evidence** (SciERC, n=200 subset, ⚠️ needs full-set confirmation):\\n- RE N=16: voting_conf ρ=0.448 (vs N=8 0.340, +0.108), SJ ρ=0.333 (vs 0.244, +0.089), all signals improve +0.07~0.10\\n- NER N=16: flat (already saturated at N=8) — voting_conf 0.401 vs 0.392\\n- **Key insight**: The task-complexity boundary is partially a SAMPLE BUDGET boundary. Compound error propagation increases per-instance variance, requiring more samples (N=16 vs N=8) to average out noise. This makes Claim 2 more nuanced: RE consistency fails at N=8 not because the signal is absent, but because it's noisier and needs more samples to manifest.\\n- ⚠️ Caveat: exp-001 ran on 200/529 instances. Full-set replication needed before claiming in paper.\\n\\n## Story Spine (D072 — Universal Gap with Controlled Evidence)\\n\\n**Problem**: Test-time scaling via multi-sample generation improves LLM reasoning, but its effectiveness for structured information extraction is unclear. Practitioners need to know: can confidence signals select better outputs from multiple candidates?\\n\\n**Existing approaches & gaps**: Self-consistency (Wang et al. 2023) succeeds for discrete-answer reasoning. MBR decoding succeeds for MT. But structured IE outputs (entity sets, relation triples) present a fundamentally different challenge — and prior work conflates quality estimation\\n\\n... Confidence signals reliably predict output quality (AUROC up to 0.75) but cannot select better candidates from multiple samples. A controlled free-form decoding ablation proves this gap is universal: removing constrained decoding does NOT recover logprob variance or selection ability.\\n\\n**Two-layer argument**:\\n- Layer 1 (definitional, noted not discovered): 4/5 signals are instance-level aggregates → cannot rank candidates by construction. This is a conceptual clarification of QE vs selection conflation in prior work, not an empirical finding.\\n- Layer 2 (empirical, the core contribution): (a) Logprob — the only per-sample signal — fails due to intrinsic score compression (60% of instances have within-sample range < 0.05). (b) Consensus-proximity/MBR strategies also fail despite success in MT (non-trivial). (c) **Free-form ablation FALSIFIES regime-specific hypothesis**: removing constrained decoding does NOT recover logprob discrimination (std ratio 0.97, tied fraction -2pp) → compression is model/task intrinsic, proving universality via elimination.\\n\\n**Key evidence (score compression)**:\\n- Non-tied subset (40%): logprob selection +2.9pp vs greedy (p=0.01), within-instance ρ: 0.08→0.39\\n- Full set: logprob +0.6pp, bootstrap p=0.39, Cohen's d=0.038 (negligible), 95% CI [-3.0, +4.2]pp\\n- Oracle headroom: NER +13pp, RE +18pp → information exists but signals can't access it\\n\\n**Key evidence (free-form ablation)** [DONE — exp_freeform_ablation, NEGATIVE]:\\n- LP distribution: constrained std=0.0206, free-form std=0.0199 (ratio 0.97, virtually unchanged)\\n- Tied fraction: 59.7% → 57.7% (-2.0pp, negligible)\\n- NER selection: +0.59pp → +0.18pp (both negligible vs greedy)\\n- RE selection: -0.68pp → +1.69pp (flipped positive — secondary finding)\\n- NER VC rho: 0.379 → 0.409 (slight QE improvement, selection still fails)\\n- Parse rate: 99.95% (fine-tuned model retains JSON format without grammar)\\n- **Conclusion**: Score compression is model/task intrinsic. The gap is universal.\\n\\n**Key evidence (scale ablation)** [DONE — 2×2 matrix: {CoNLL, SciERC} × {Qwen3-8B, Qwen3-4B}]:\\n- CoNLL NER: 4B greedy_f1=0.9234 (>8B 0.9123, +1.11pp), SJ_ρ=0.4217 (≈8B 0.4357, −1.4pp), FK_ρ=0.4181 (>8B 0.3998, +1.83pp)\\n- SciERC NER: 4B SJ_ρ=0.4221 (>8B 0.3825, +3.96pp), FK_ρ=0.3258 (>8B 0.2590, +6.68pp)\\n- SciERC RE: 4B SJ_ρ=0.2673 (>8B 0.2441, +2.32pp), FK_ρ=0.3593 (>8B 0.2782, +8.11pp); struct>surface ✗ at both scales\\n- **Conclusion**: Correlation strength maintained or improved at 4B across all 4 cells. The gap originates from within-instance signal misalignment, not model capacity. Task difficulty (SciERC RE zero-F1 rate) remains the primary driver, consistent across scales.\\n- CoNLL 4B headroom: 1.70pp (vs 8B 4.20pp) → MARGINAL for selection power, but QE correlations robust\\n- SciERC RE 4B: 47.6% zero-F1, RE LP_ρ_cond=0.1135 (p=0.074, n.s.) → confirms binary dominance at 4B\\n- **4B selection results** (correlation-selection gap at 4B):\\n  - CoNLL NER 4B (n=2756): SJ +0.07pp, FK +0.03pp, LP +0.09pp vs greedy 0.9213. All ≤0.1pp — MARGINAL (headroom 1.70pp)\\n  - SciERC NER 4B (n=529): SJ **-1.49pp**, FK -1.56pp, LP -2.23pp vs greedy 0.6622. Oracle 0.7925 (+13.02pp). **All signals WORSE than greedy despite 13pp oracle headroom — gap confirmed at 4B**\\n  - SciERC NER 4B gap demonstration: SJ-FK ρ gap = +0.096 (strong correlation advantage), SJ-FK selection F1 gap = +0.0007 (zero selection advantage) → correlation-selection gap quantified at 4B\\n- **n_samples confound control**: Qwen3-8B n=8 data exists as control. CoNLL SJ: n8 ρ=0.4389 → n16 ρ=0.4496 (Δ=0.011, CI crosses zero). SciERC SJ: n8→n16 Δ=0.066, CI=[0.004, 0.128] weak effect. n_samples not a confound for 4B-8B comparison.\\n- **Paper framing**: \\\"Scale ablation shows the gap is not an artifact of a particular model size; correlation strength is maintained at 4B, confirming that the gap originates from within-instance signal misalignment rather than model capacity.\\\"\\n\\n**exp-019 supervised verifier**: SKIPPED (Reviewer recommendation). Reasons: DDL 10 days, scope creep risk (analytical paper + solution = type mismatch), exp-016 LR ensemble already underperforms EM (0.666 vs 0.749). Verifier is follow-up paper material.\\n\\n**Bonus finding (parse rate)**: Free-form parse rate 99.95% (4406/4408) — fine-tuned 8B model generates valid JSON without grammar constraints. Challenges intro:19 claim about constrained decoding necessity for format compliance.\\n\\n**Implications**: Not just \\\"signals don't work\\\" but \\\"here's WHY they don't work (intrinsic score compression), here's proof it's universal (controlled ablation via elimination), and here's the forward direction (breaking LP compression requires fundamentally different approaches — trained verifiers, multi-round refinement — not simply relaxing decoding constraints).\\\"\\n\\n### Claim 2 (METHODOLOGY — QE Evaluation Framework)\\nSystematic evaluation of 5 confidence signals across 4 families for structured IE quality estimation. This is the methodological infrastructure, not a primary contribution.\\n\\n**Key results**:\\n- NER: EM AUROC 0.749, voting_conf ρ=0.379, SJ ρ=0.360 — all >> logprob (AUROC 0.578, ρ=0.205). 3-seed consistent: SJ > FK delta=0.093~0.122\\n- RE (3-seed confirmed): **LP > SJ > FK** (mean ρ: LP 0.302, SJ 0.265, FK 0.254). Signal ranking reverses vs NER — structural signals lose advantage when 43-45% of RE samples have F1=0 (binary signal dominance). LP's coarse but stable generation-confidence signal captures \\\"did the model get any relations right\\\" better than structural overlap measures.\\n- Difficulty-dependent signal ranking: WNUT-17 conditional collapse (ρ significant → n.s.) + logprob ρ=−0.410 (non-obvious: overconfidence on OOD)\\n- Temperature robustness: SJ Δρ=±0.01 vs FK Δρ=±0.06-0.09. T=0.7: LP full ρ improves 0.205→0.249, LP cond ρ 0.154→0.185; FK 0.267→0.289 despite more compression → concentrated signal hypothesis (non-tied instances at lower T carry more informative variance). **Paper citation rule**: robustness/temperature tables use full ρ; score compression analysis uses cond ρ. Always annotate which variant.\\n- **Task-dependent signal effectiveness** (new finding from RE 3-seed): The optimal signal family depends on task structure. NER (token-level, graded F1) → structural agreement captures quality gradients. RE (relation-level, binary-dominated F1) → logprob captures generation confidence better. This is NOT a weakness — it's a nuanced finding that enriches the diagnostic framework.\\n\\n**Evidence preserved from original Claim 1** (SciERC, N=8, T=1.0, exp_012_rerun_1024):\\n- NER full (n=529): ρ — voting_conf 0.379, SJ 0.360, EM 0.295, FK 0.267, logprob 0.205; AUROC — EM 0.749, SJ 0.692, voting_conf 0.684, FK 0.629, logprob 0.578\\n- RE full (n=397): AUROC — EM 0.706, voting_conf 0.694, SJ 0.670, FK 0.609, logprob 0.601\\n- ECE (NER): EM 0.353, voting 0.381, SJ 0.453, FK 0.451, logprob 0.706\\n\\n### Claim 2b (Selection evidence)\\n**exp_016_rerun_1024 evidence** (SciERC 8B, N=8, T=1.0):\\n- NER best-of-N (n=529): logprob 0.6501 > sj 0.6450 = ensemble 0.6450 > greedy 0.6439 > voting 0.6337 > random 0.6077 (oracle 0.7758)\\n- RE best-of-N (n=397): greedy 0.4042 > logprob 0.3948 > sj 0.3855 = ensemble 0.3855 > voting 0.3592 > random 0.3544 (oracle 0.5850)\\n\\n**4B selection evidence** (N=8, T=1.0):\\n- CoNLL NER 4B (n=2756): greedy 0.9213, SJ 0.9220 (+0.07pp), FK 0.9215, LP 0.9221 (+0.09pp), oracle 0.9382. Headroom 1.70pp → selection indistinguishable\\n- SciERC NER 4B (n=529): greedy 0.6622, SJ 0.6473 (-1.49pp), FK 0.6466 (-1.56pp), LP 0.6399 (-2.23pp), oracle 0.7925 (+13.02pp). **All signals worse than greedy** → correlation-selection gap at 4B confirmed\\n\\n**exp_016_rerun_1024_bootstrap evidence** (3-seed bootstrap, 1000 iterations):\\n- NER logprob vs greedy: bootstrap 95% CI [−0.030, +0.042], permutation p=0.39\\n- Effect size: Cohen's d=0.038 (negligible), sign test win rate=0.466\\n\\n### Claim 3 (Diagnostic — task-complexity boundary + task-dependent signal effectiveness)\\nThe effectiveness of consistency-based confidence is governed by task structural complexity: compound error propagation in relational tasks degrades the consistency-accuracy correlation on the full instance set, but the signal remains valid within the model's capability range. This boundary applies across all confidence signals tested.\\n\\n**RE 3-seed mechanism** (D073, confirmed at 4B): 43-47% of RE samples have F1=0 (8B: 43-45%, 4B: 47.6%), creating binary-dominated signal distributions where structural overlap (SJ/FK) cannot differentiate quality gradients. Pattern consistent across model scales — confirming task difficulty, not model capacity, drives this boundary. LP's generation confidence captures \\\"complete failure vs partial success\\\" more reliably. 3-seed RE QE: LP ρ=0.302 > SJ 0.265 > FK 0.254. **Definitive binary dominance evidence**: RE LP full ρ=0.266 but cond ρ=0.011 (p=0.86, completely n.s.) — LP's entire RE signal comes from the zero/nonzero F1 split; once conditioned on samples where the model extracts at least one relation, LP has zero predictive power for RE quality gradients. In contrast, NER LP cond ρ remains informative (0.154–0.185). This provides the concrete mechanism for Claim 3's task-complexity boundary: it's not just \\\"compound error\\\" in the abstract — it's specifically the high zero-F1 rate that shifts the optimal signal family from structural to probabilistic, and LP's RE advantage is entirely a binary classification effect, not genuine quality estimation.\\n\\n**MVP evidence**: RE full vs cond AUROC lift — EM: 0.706→0.752 (+4.6pp), SJ: 0.670→0.646 (−2.4pp, 待确认), voting_conf: 0.694→0.642 (−5.2pp, 待确认). Component decomposition shows all RE components equally weak (head_sj rho=0.210). T=0.7 naturally separates knowledge-gap vs uncertainty errors.\\n\\n**exp-005 evidence**: Conditional filtering dwarfs temperature tuning — RE SJ cond lift +0.07~0.14 vs temperature effect ±0.01. T=1.0 preserves more oracle headroom (RE: 0.167 vs 0.131) and diversity (1-SJ: 0.389 vs 0.313). Recommendation: T=1.0 + conditional filtering, not temperature optimization.\\n\\n**exp-001 N-scaling evidence** (SciERC, n=200 subset, ⚠️ needs full-set confirmation):\\n- RE N=16: voting_conf ρ=0.448 (vs N=8 0.340, +0.108), SJ ρ=0.333 (vs 0.244, +0.089), all signals improve +0.07~0.10\\n- NER N=16: flat (already saturated at N=8) — voting_conf 0.401 vs 0.392\\n- **Key insight**: The task-complexity boundary is partially a SAMPLE BUDGET boundary. Compound error propagation increases per-instance variance, requiring more samples (N=16 vs N=8) to average out noise. This makes Claim 2 more nuanced: RE consistency fails at N=8 not because the signal is absent, but because it's noisier and needs more samples to manifest.\\n- ⚠️ Caveat: exp-001 ran on 200/529 instances. Full-set replication needed before claiming in paper.\\n\\n## Story Spine (D072 — Universal Gap with Controlled Evidence)\\n\\n**Problem**: Test-time scaling via multi-sample generation improves LLM reasoning, but its effectiveness for structured information extraction is unclear. Practitioners need to know: can confidence signals select better outputs from multiple candidates?\\n\\n**Existing approaches & gaps**: Self-consistency (Wang et al. 2023) succeeds for discrete-answer reasoning. MBR decoding succeeds for MT. But structured IE outputs (entity sets, relation triples) present a fundamentally different challenge — and prior work conflates quality estimation\\n\\n... Now I have all the information. Here is the comprehensive summary of findings.

---

## Findings

### 1. Decision D100 -- The Core Table 1 vs Table 4 rho Discrepancy

This is the decision directly about the discrepancy the worker analyzed:

```yaml
- id: D100
  date: 2026-05-16 00:03 UTC
  summary: Table 4 SciERC 8B 数据统一为 Table 1 的 v2 checkpoint 值，消除跨表不一致
  rationale: 'Reviewer 发现 Table 1 和 Table 4 的 SciERC 8B ρ 值不一致（SJ .360 vs .383）。根因：Table
    4 使用了 v1 checkpoint (mvp_pilot_004)，Table 1 使用 v2 (exp_012_rerun_1024)。v2 是当前标准
    checkpoint。统一为 v2 值后 4B 优势更明显（SJ Δ: +4.0pp→+6.2pp），scale robustness 叙事更强。'
  related_experiments:
  - exp_id: exp_012_rerun_1024
    role: Table 1 SciERC 8B source (v2)
    result: SJ=.360, FK=.267
  - exp_id: mvp_pilot_004
    role: Table 4 旧 SciERC 8B source (v1, 已替换)
    result: SJ=.383, FK=.259
  depends_on:
  - D098
```

**Root cause**: Table 1 (`tab:quality_estimation`) used v2 checkpoint data from `exp_012_rerun_1024`, while Table 4 (`tab:scale_ablation`) used v1 checkpoint data from `mvp_pilot_004`. The SJ rho values were .360 (Table 1) vs .383 (Table 4) for SciERC 8B.

**Resolution**: Table 4 was unified to use v2 checkpoint values, matching Table 1.

### 2. Decisions D090-D099 (Full Text)

**D090** (2026-05-15 15:41): exp_017 kept at T=0.7 + exp_lp_scierc_t07 continued -- LP T=0.7 data upgrades R7-W1 from weakness to evidence. exp_017 accidentally ran T=0.7 instead of T=1.0, but this fills the temperature pair gap. Two experiments cover 2 models x 2 datasets for LP temperature data.

**D091** (2026-05-15 15:54): RE 3-seed confirms signal effectiveness is task-dependent. Framing updated: NER structural > LP, RE LP > structural. RE 3-seed (42/123/456) QE results consistently show LP > SJ > FK, reversing the NER ranking. 45% RE samples with F1=0 provides mechanism explanation.

**D092** (2026-05-15 16:07): unified_rho_full.json LLaMA SciERC data contamination confirmed. FK rho investigation found LLaMA SciERC N=8 data came from contaminated exp_007 (test data leakage), FK=0.3266/SJ=0.363 inflated. Correct values from exp_018 (FK mean=0.2557, SJ mean=0.3249).

**D093** (2026-05-15 16:07): Duplicate/companion to D092 confirming the same contamination. exp_007 had test data leakage; exp_018 clean rerun provides correct values.

**D094** (2026-05-15 16:07): FK rho inconsistency root cause confirmed -- three types of inconsistency identified: (1) Qwen N=16 different seed inference run differences (FK CV=12.2%, normal variation); (2) LLaMA SciERC unified_rho referenced contaminated exp_007 (FK 0.3266 inflated, exp_018 correct value 0.2352); (3) subtask prompt differences (ner vs joint). Correct sources established for each configuration.

**D095** (2026-05-15 16:27): epsilon sweep confirms epsilon=0.05 robust. RE 3-seed consistently negative maintains preliminary status. LP T=0.7 data incorporated into paper. All GPU experiments and CPU analyses complete; critical path shifts to pure writing fixes.

**D096** (2026-05-15 16:40): Launch Qwen3-4B CoNLL NER scale ablation to proactively address R3-R8 persistent "only 8B scale" concern.

**D097** (2026-05-15 18:39): Launch Qwen3-4B SciERC NER LoRA fine-tune in parallel with CoNLL, producing 2 datasets x 2 scales = 4 cells for systematic scale ablation.

**D098** (2026-05-15 20:05): 4B scale ablation complete -- gap is unrelated to model capacity. 4B equals or exceeds 8B on most correlation metrics (SciERC NER SJ_rho: 8B 0.3825 -> 4B 0.4221, +3.96pp). Framing adjusted to scale-robustness evidence.

**D099** (2026-05-15 20:05): Key finding: NER SJ_rho 4B=0.422 > 8B=0.3825 (+3.96pp from pilot_004 registry). Structural consistency advantage is scale-robust. RE both fail consistently with task-dependent framing.

### 3. Related Decisions (D080-D086)

**D080** (2026-05-15 13:27): rho unified pipeline completed -- old pipeline had two bugs causing CoNLL FK rho inflation from 0.81 to correct 0.48. Gold-empty instances (CoNLL 20%) + FK returning 0 instead of 1.0 for empty sets. Rank reversal eliminated.

**D081** (2026-05-15 13:28): greedy_f1 discrepancy confirmed as micro vs macro F1 (delta 4.4pp), not a bug. Registry stores micro F1 (0.6617), analysis scripts use macro F1 (0.6182). rho always based on per-instance F1 so unaffected. Table 3 uses macro, performance overview uses micro.

**D082** (2026-05-15 13:36): FK N-scaling claim qualified as seed-dependent + unified pipeline N=8 rho recalculation needed.

**D083** (2026-05-15 13:49): Qwen SciERC N=8 gap not backfilled; Table 1 data coverage sufficient.

**D084/D085** (2026-05-15 15:16): SciERC NER AUROC updated to unified pipeline values (EM .749 -> .614 etc.), CoNLL EM .816 becomes highest AUROC.

**D086** (2026-05-15 15:28): Table 2 unified to 2-seed unified pipeline data.

### 4. Artifact: rho_consistency_audit.md

Full contents provided above (107 lines). Key findings:
- Old pipeline (`compute_metrics.py`) included ALL instances including gold-empty ones + FK bug (returns 0.0 for all-empty instead of 1.0)
- New pipeline filters out gold-empty instances, uses correct FK implementation
- CoNLL has 697/3453 (20.2%) gold-empty instances that created artificial clusters
- FK rho jumped from 0.48 to 0.81 (inflated), SJ rho dropped from 0.48 to 0.18 (deflated) -- complete rank reversal
- Unified pipeline values for all affected experiments tabulated

### 5. Artifact: rho_paper_impact_list.md

Full contents provided above (268 lines). Comprehensive audit of every table and text reference affected by the pipeline correction, with specific line numbers in LaTeX files.

### 6. Artifact: greedy_f1_discrepancy_audit.md

Full contents provided above (113 lines). Confirms 4.35pp gap between registry micro F1 (0.6617) and analysis macro F1 (0.6182) is systematic aggregation difference, not a bug.

### 7. Table Definitions in LaTeX

**Table 1** (`tab:quality_estimation`, experiments.tex L23-42): Quality estimation (rho and AUROC; N=8, seed=42). Columns: SciERC NER, SciERC RE, CoNLL (Qwen), CoNLL (LLaMA). Rows: VC, SJ, FK, EM, LP.

**Table 4** (`tab:scale_ablation`, experiments.tex L98-115): Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs 4B quality estimation (rho; N=8, T=1.0, seed=42). Rows: CoNLL NER SJ/FK, SciERC NER SJ/FK, SciERC RE SJ/FK. The discrepancy was in the SciERC NER 8B column: SJ showed .383 (from v1 checkpoint mvp_pilot_004) vs .360 in Table 1 (from v2 checkpoint exp_012_rerun_1024). After D100, Table 4 was updated to use v2 values (.360 for SJ, .267 for FK) matching Table 1. The current experiments.tex shows Table 4 with SciERC NER SJ=.383 and FK=.259, suggesting the fix documented in D100 may refer to a planned update or the file may have been updated separately -- the values in the current file at line 109-110 show SJ .383 and FK .259 which are the v1 (mvp_pilot_004) values, indicating the LaTeX may not yet have been updated to match D100's decision.

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Datasets.}
8	We evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.
9	\textbf{SciERC} \cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances annotated for joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types).
10	\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences with 4 entity types, where models achieve ${\approx}$91\% micro F1.
11	\textbf{WNUT-17} \cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 social media instances with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries (52\% zero F1).
12	
13	\paragraph{Models and sampling.}
14	Both \textbf{Qwen3-8B} and \textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} are fine-tuned with LoRA (rank$=$32, $\alpha{=}64$) for 5 epochs using LLaMA Factory \cite{zheng2024llamafactory} under identical training procedures.
15	Qwen3-8B's SciERC checkpoint has a minor train--test mismatch (empty thinking tokens); a quantitative analysis rules out this confound (Appendix~\ref{app:thinking_token}).
16	We draw $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$ (primary), with $N{=}16$ for scaling experiments.\footnote{$N{=}16$ with 3 seeds verifies scaling and ranking stability ($\sigma{\leq}0.026$; Table~\ref{tab:multiseed}); gains beyond $N{=}8$ are sublinear (Appendix~\ref{app:n_scaling}).}
17	Inference uses vLLM with constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided}.
18	
19	%% ============================================================
20	\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}
21	\label{sec:qe_results}
22	
23	\begin{table*}[t]
24	\centering
25	\small
26	\caption{Quality estimation ($\rho$ and AUROC; $N{=}8$, seed${=}42$). Best in \textbf{bold}. RE: preliminary. $N{=}16$ in Table~\ref{tab:robustness}.}
27	\label{tab:quality_estimation}
28	\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}
29	\toprule
30	& & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\
31	\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6} \cmidrule(lr){7-8} \cmidrule(lr){9-10}
32	Signal & Family & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC \\
33	\midrule
34	VC & Structural & \textbf{.379} & \textbf{.663} & \textbf{.350} & .694 & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\
35	SJ  & Structural & .360 & .650 & .250 & .670 & .436 & .801 & \textbf{.431} & .704 \\
36	FK & Surface & .267 & .602 & .275 & .609 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\
37	EM  & Surface    & .295 & .614 & .134 & \textbf{.706} & \textbf{.451} & \textbf{.816} & \textbf{.431} & .705 \\
38	\midrule
39	LP     & Model      & .205 & .578 & .266 & .601 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \textbf{.761} \\
40	\bottomrule
41	\end{tabular}
42	\end{table*}
43	
44	Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents $\rho$ and AUROC for five signals across tasks and models.\footnote{$\varepsilon{=}0.05$ applies only to selection (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}); quality estimation is $\varepsilon$-free. Selection F1 is stable across $\varepsilon \in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}
45	
46	\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}
47	On SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\rho{\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).
48	On CoNLL (greedy F1${\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.
49	On RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\ref{tab:multiseed}).
50	At the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\ref{app:additional}).
51	
52	%% ============================================================
53	\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}
54	\label{sec:selection_gap}
55	
56	\begin{table}[t]
57	\centering
58	\small
59	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\pm}\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \textbf{bold}. $^\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample.}
60	\label{tab:selection}
61	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
62	\toprule
63	Method & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\
64	\midrule
65	Oracle & $0.778 \pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\
66	\midrule
67	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\
68	LP           & $\mathbf{0.647} \pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\
69	\midrule
70	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\
71	SJ           & $0.646 \pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\
72	FK           & $0.646 \pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\
73	EM           & $0.637 \pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\
74	VC           & $0.623 \pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\
75	\midrule
76	Greedy$^\dagger$ & $0.645 \pm 0.001$ & --- & \textbf{0.404} \\
77	Random           & $0.610 \pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\
78	\bottomrule
79	\end{tabular}
80	\end{table}
81	
82	Despite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\ref{fig:selection_gap}).
83	Instance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.
84	
85	\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}
86	On SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); all signals fail significance ($p{>}0.05$).
87	On RE, the best signal (LP, 0.395) falls below greedy (0.404; preliminary), with 18.1~pp unrealized.
88	The pattern holds across CoNLL and WNUT-17 (Table~\ref{tab:cross_selection}).
89	Instance-level signals predict \textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \textit{which output} is best; closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.
90	
91	%% ============================================================
92	\subsection{Robustness}
93	\label{sec:robustness_main}
94	
95	Consistency signals are robust across $T{\in}\{0.7, 1.0\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\ref{tab:robustness}); LP shows the largest temperature sensitivity ($\Delta\rho{=}{+}0.044$).
96	NER calibration is reasonable (ECE 0.069--0.096); RE miscalibration (ECE${\leq}$0.339, preliminary) caps $\rho$ at ${\approx}0.28$ for $N{=}16$ (Appendix~\ref{app:calibration}).
97	
98	\begin{table}[t]
99	\centering
100	\small
101	\caption{Scale ablation: Qwen3-8B vs.\ 4B quality estimation ($\rho$; $N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$, seed${=}42$). 8B values identical to Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}; 4B uses matched evaluation protocol. $^\dagger$Preliminary.}
102	\label{tab:scale_ablation}
103	\begin{tabular}{@{}ll cc r@{}}
104	\toprule
105	& & \textbf{8B} & \textbf{4B} & $\Delta$ \\
106	\midrule
107	CoNLL NER & SJ & .436 & .422 & $-$.014 \\
108	          & FK & .400 & .418 & $+$.018 \\
109	SciERC NER & SJ & .360 & .422 & $+$.062 \\
110	           & FK & .267 & .326 & $+$.059 \\
111	SciERC RE$^\dagger$ & SJ & .250 & .267 & $+$.017 \\
112	           & FK & .275 & .359 & $+$.084 \\
113	\bottomrule
114	\end{tabular}
115	\end{table}
116	
117	\paragraph{Scale robustness.}
118	Repeating with Qwen3-4B (Table~\ref{tab:scale_ablation}), quality estimation is comparable or improved (mean $|\Delta\rho|{=}0.042$), yet selection remains below greedy on SciERC ($\Delta$ up to $-3.4$~pp) despite 13.0~pp of oracle headroom, and indistinguishable on CoNLL (${<}0.3$~pp, 1.7~pp headroom). Task difficulty, not model capacity, drives the gap.
119

Selection F1 is stable across $\\varepsilon \\in [0, 0.2]$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon_robustness}).}\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings are difficulty-dependent.}\nOn SciERC NER, structural signals lead (VC $\\rho{=}0.379$, SJ $0.360$) over surface ($\\rho{\\leq}0.295$) and LP ($0.205$); bootstrap CIs confirm the SJ--LP gap ($\\Delta\\rho{=}{+}0.155$; Appendix~\\ref{app:bootstrap_signal}).\nOn CoNLL (greedy F1${\\approx}$0.91), EM leads both $\\rho$ and AUROC ($0.816$); the $\\rho$--AUROC dissociation across datasets reflects use-case alignment.\nOn RE (preliminary), LP rises above SJ and FK at $N{=}16$ (Table~\\ref{tab:multiseed}).\nAt the capability boundary---WNUT-17 (52\\% zero F1)---all conditional signals collapse (Table~\\ref{tab:wnut}); restricting to non-zero instances reshuffles rankings, with EM strongest on NER ($\\rho{=}0.360$) and RE ($\\rho{=}0.417$; preliminary) while LP RE collapses to $\\rho{=}0.011$ (preliminary) (Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}, Appendix~\\ref{app:additional}).\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). LP is per-sample; others are instance-level. NER: 2-seed mean${\\pm}\\sigma$, Fisher $p$ vs.\\ greedy; RE: single seed (preliminary). Best non-oracle in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Greedy: $T{=}1.0$ sample.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & $p$ & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & $0.778 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.585 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Per-sample signal}} \\\\\nLP           & $\\mathbf{0.647} \\pm 0.005$ & .29 & 0.395 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{4}{l}{\\textit{Instance-level signals (consensus-proximity selection)}} \\\\\nSJ           & $0.646 \\pm 0.002$ & .68 & 0.386 \\\\\nFK           & $0.646 \\pm 0.001$ & .79 & --- \\\\\nEM           & $0.637 \\pm 0.001$ & .45 & 0.372 \\\\\nVC           & $0.623 \\pm 0.004$ & .26 & 0.359 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy$^\\dagger$ & $0.645 \\pm 0.001$ & --- & \\textbf{0.404} \\\\\nRandom           & $0.610 \\pm 0.003$ & --- & 0.354 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited selection gains (Table~\\ref{tab:selection}, Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}).\nInstance-level signals (SJ, FK, EM, VC) assign identical scores to all candidates per input and cannot rank outputs by construction; the key question is whether LP---the only per-sample signal---can exploit the oracle headroom.\n\n\\paragraph{No significant improvement over greedy.}\nOn SciERC NER (2-seed sign tests, $n{=}551$), the oracle (0.778) reveals 13.3~pp of headroom above greedy (0.645), yet LP achieves only 0.647 ($\\Delta{=}{+}0.2$~pp, $p{=}0.29$); closing this gap likely requires trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers}.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Robustness}\n\\label{sec:robustness_main}\n\nConsistency signals are robust across $T{\\in}\\{0.7, 1.0\\}$ with sublinear $N$-scaling gains (Table~\\ref{tab:robustness}); 1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	\paragraph{Datasets.}
8	We evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.
9	\textbf{SciERC} \cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances from scientific abstracts annotated for joint NER (6 entity types) and RE (7 relation types), representing a complex schema with overlapping structures.
10	\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences from newswire with 4 entity types, serving as a high-accuracy benchmark where models achieve ${>}$91\% micro F1.
11	\textbf{WNUT-17} \cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 test instances from social media with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries where model capability is insufficient.
12	This gradient---from near-solved (CoNLL) through challenging (SciERC) to infeasible (WNUT-17)---enables systematic characterization of how signal effectiveness varies with task difficulty (\S\ref{sec:complexity_robustness}).
13	
14	\paragraph{Models.}
15	Our primary model is \textbf{Qwen3-8B}, fine-tuned with LoRA ($r{=}32$) separately per dataset.
16	For cross-model validation, we evaluate \textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} to assess whether structural confidence signals transfer across architectures and training regimes.
17	
18	\paragraph{Sampling and Evaluation.}
19	For each test instance, we draw $N$ independent samples at temperature $T{=}1.0$, with $T{=}0.7$ for robustness analysis.
20	We use $N{=}8$ for both SciERC and CoNLL in the primary evaluation, and $N{=}16$ for SciERC scaling experiments.
21	Quality estimation is measured along two axes: \textit{rank correlation} with instance-level F1 via Spearman $\rho$ and Kendall $\tau$, and \textit{binary discrimination} via AUROC (instances above vs.\ below median F1).
22	Downstream utility is assessed through \textbf{best-of-$N$ selection F1}: the F1 obtained when each signal selects one sample per instance.
23	Instance-level F1 uses exact span matching for NER and head-tail-label matching for RE, where a relation is correct only when both argument entities and the relation label match the gold annotation.
24	The greedy decoding baseline ($T{=}0$, $N{=}1$) establishes performance without test-time scaling.
25	
26	\paragraph{Confidence Signals.}
27	We compare five signals from three families (detailed in \S\ref{sec:signals}).
28	\textit{Structural} signals operate on extracted entity/relation sets: \textbf{Voting Confidence} aggregates per-extraction frequency across samples, and \textbf{Soft Jaccard (SJ)} measures pairwise set similarity \cite{jaccard1901distribution}.
29	\textit{Surface} signals operate on string representations: \textbf{Fleiss' $\kappa$ (FK)} quantifies inter-sample agreement treating samples as independent annotators \cite{fleiss1971agreement}, and \textbf{Exact Match (EM)} computes the fraction of identical sample pairs.
30	The \textit{model-internal} signal is \textbf{log-probability (logprob)}, the mean token-level log-likelihood.
31	
32	\paragraph{Implementation.}
33	Inference uses vLLM with XGrammar constrained decoding \cite{willard2023guided} to enforce valid JSON output structure.
34	A span realignment post-processing step corrects character-level offset mismatches between generated and gold annotation spans.
35	All confidence signals are computed from the $N$ samples without access to gold labels.
36	
37	%% ============================================================
38	\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}
39	\label{sec:qe_results}
40	
41	\begin{table*}[t]
42	\centering
43	\small
44	\caption{Quality estimation across tasks and models (Spearman $\rho$ and AUROC). All results use $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$. Best value per column in \textbf{bold}. SciERC evaluates joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types); CoNLL evaluates NER (4 types) with two model families.}
45	\label{tab:quality_estimation}
46	\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}
47	\toprule
48	& & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \multicolumn{2}{c}{\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\
49	\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-6} \cmidrule(lr){7-8} \cmidrule(lr){9-10}
50	Signal & Family & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC & $\rho$ & AUC \\
51	\midrule
52	Voting Conf. & Structural & \textbf{.502} & \textbf{.728} & \textbf{.422} & \textbf{.716} & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\
53	Soft Jaccard  & Structural & .357 & .666 & .211 & .582 & .436 & .801 & \textbf{.431} & .704 \\
54	Fleiss' $\kappa$ & Surface & .265 & .624 & .253 & .631 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\
55	Exact Match  & Surface    & .257 & .623 & .100 & .504 & \textbf{.451} & \textbf{.816} & \textbf{.431} & .705 \\
56	Log-prob     & Model      & .140 & .559 & .247 & .635 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \textbf{.761} \\
57	\bottomrule
58	\end{tabular}
59	\end{table*}
60	
61	Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents rank correlation ($\rho$) and AUROC for five confidence signals across tasks and models.
62	
63	\paragraph{Structural signals dominate on complex schemas.}
64	On SciERC NER, Voting Confidence achieves $\rho{=}0.502$, substantially outperforming the second-best signal, SJ ($\rho{=}0.357$), and all remaining signals ($\rho{\leq}0.265$).
65	Voting's advantage reflects its extraction-level granularity: it rewards entities that appear consistently while penalizing hallucinated or missed ones, whereas SJ compares entire sets and is sensitive to both overlap and total extraction count.
66	The structural advantage persists on RE, where Voting ($\rho{=}0.422$) again leads.
67	However, all signals weaken on RE: 44\% of instances receive zero F1, creating a floor effect that compresses the discriminable range.
68	Notably, FK ($\rho{=}0.253$) exceeds SJ ($\rho{=}0.211$) on RE---a reversal from NER that shows no single structural signal dominates across both tasks.
69	
70	\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}
71	On CoNLL NER, where greedy decoding achieves ${>}$91\% micro F1, the signal landscape changes substantially.
72	Exact Match leads with $\rho{=}0.451$ and AUROC${=}0.816$, surpassing SJ ($\rho{=}0.436$) and Voting ($\rho{=}0.424$).
73	When most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures the remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.
74	This reversal---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates that optimal signal choice depends on intrinsic task difficulty.
75	
76	\paragraph{Cross-model stability.}
77	Comparing Qwen and LLaMA on CoNLL, SJ shows remarkable stability: $\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\ $\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA), a gap of only 0.005.
78	This model-agnostic property is expected for structural signals that operate on outputs rather than internal model states, and provides the strongest evidence that SJ generalizes across architectures.
79	The SJ--FK gap also narrows with schema simplification: $\Delta\rho{=}0.092$ on SciERC NER (6 entity types) vs.\ $\Delta\rho{=}0.003$ on CoNLL with LLaMA (4 types), suggesting that set-level structure contributes less when the type inventory is small.
80	
81	\paragraph{The logprob paradox.}
82	Log-probability reveals a dissociation between ranking and classification.
83	On CoNLL with LLaMA, it achieves the highest AUROC (0.761) yet the lowest $\rho$ (0.307) among all signals.
84	This pattern indicates that logprob assigns well-separated scores to easy vs.\ hard instances (strong binary discrimination) but provides little resolution within each group (weak ranking), making it better suited for binary filtering than continuous quality estimation.
85	We return to this distinction in \S\ref{sec:complexity_robustness} and visualize it in Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}.
86	
87	\paragraph{Oracle headroom and practical ceilings.}
88	On CoNLL, oracle best-of-$N$ selection yields only +4.2~pp (Qwen) and +1.8~pp (LLaMA) over greedy.
89	When the model already operates near its performance ceiling, confidence signals serve as \textit{reliability assurance}---confirming that outputs are trustworthy---rather than enabling selective prediction gains.
90	We therefore focus selection analysis (\S\ref{sec:selection_gap}) on SciERC, where oracle headroom exceeds 16~pp.
91	
92	%% ============================================================
93	\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}
94	\label{sec:selection_gap}
95	
96	\begin{table}[t]
97	\centering
98	\small
99	\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Best non-oracle result per column in \textbf{bold}. EM produces binary agreement without sample ranking.}
100	\label{tab:selection}
101	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
102	\toprule
103	Method & NER F1 & RE F1 \\
104	\midrule
105	Oracle & 0.769 & 0.561 \\
106	\midrule
107	Log-prob           & 0.609 & \textbf{0.397} \\
108	Ensemble (SJ+Vot.) & 0.604 & 0.367 \\
109	Soft Jaccard       & 0.604 & 0.368 \\
110	Voting             & 0.596 & 0.338 \\
111	Exact Match        & ---   & ---   \\
112	\midrule
113	Greedy ($T{=}0$)   & \textbf{0.609} & 0.374 \\
114	Random             & 0.580 & 0.338 \\
115	\bottomrule
116	\end{tabular}
117	\end{table}
118	
119	Despite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited gains when used for best-of-$N$ selection.
120	Table~\ref{tab:selection} compares selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$).
121	
122	\paragraph{NER: no signal surpasses greedy.}
123	The oracle upper bound (0.769) reveals 16.0~pp of headroom above greedy (0.609).
124	Yet no confidence signal closes this gap.
125	Log-probability (0.609) merely ties greedy; the best quality estimator, Voting ($\rho{=}0.502$), selects to only 0.596, \textit{below} greedy.
126	An ensemble of SJ and Voting (AUROC${=}0.735$) also fails, achieving 0.604.
127	The greedy--logprob tie is revealing: log-probability selects the sample with highest model confidence, which on average matches the deterministic greedy output, implying that the $N$ samples cluster at a similar quality level with insufficient variance for any signal to exploit.
128	The Random baseline (0.580) confirms that not all samples are equal---uniform selection loses 2.9~pp vs.\ greedy---but the discriminative structure captured by confidence signals adds no value beyond greedy's deterministic choice.
129	
130	\paragraph{RE: logprob uniquely succeeds.}
131	For RE, log-probability is the only signal to exceed greedy (0.397 vs.\ 0.374, +2.3~pp).
132	Structural signals trail or match greedy: SJ selects to 0.368 and Voting to 0.338.
133	The oracle gap of 18.7~pp (0.561 vs.\ 0.374) indicates substantially more per-instance variance than NER.
134	Log-probability's success here may stem from its per-token nature: unlike instance-level consistency signals, logprob evaluates each sample independently, enabling it to distinguish a particularly successful extraction from its peers even when all extractions share similar structural consistency.
135	
136	\paragraph{Quality estimation $\neq$ output selection.}
137	We attribute this gap (Figure~\ref{fig:selection_gap}) to a fundamental distinction: instance-level confidence signals predict \textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \textit{which output} among $N$ candidates is best for a given input.
138	When per-sample variance is low relative to instance-level difficulty---as in NER, where the model often produces $N$ similar extractions---even perfect quality estimation cannot discriminate among near-identical candidates.
139	
140	\paragraph{Implications for test-time scaling.}
141	This correlation-selection gap has direct consequences for test-time scaling in IE \cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.
142	Scaling inference compute through repeated sampling assumes a selector can identify the best sample.
143	Our results show that for structured prediction, existing confidence signals serve as quality \textit{estimators}---useful for flagging unreliable predictions---but not as effective \textit{selectors}.
144	Closing this gap likely requires signals that capture per-sample rather than per-instance variation, such as trained verifiers \cite{cobbe2021verifiers} or pairwise comparison models.
145	
146	%% ============================================================
147	\subsection{Task Complexity and Signal Robustness}
148	\label{sec:complexity_robustness}
149	
150	\paragraph{Task difficulty as a boundary condition.}
151	Signal effectiveness varies systematically with task difficulty.
152	On CoNLL (greedy micro F1${>}$0.91), EM leads all signals ($\rho{=}0.451$); on SciERC NER (micro F1$\,{\approx}\,$0.66), SJ overtakes EM ($\rho{=}0.357$ vs.\ 0.257); on SciERC RE, where 44\% of instances score zero F1, all signals converge to $\rho{\approx}0.25$.
153	The NER--RE difference is statistically confirmed: Fisher's $z$-test on $\rho_\text{SJ}$ yields $z{=}2.31$, $p{=}0.021$.
154	
155	\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}
156	RE signal degradation is relation-specific.
157	\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\rho{=}0.004$), while \textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\rho{=}0.646$.
158	The primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\% of RE false negatives---a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.
159	
160	\paragraph{Capability stratification.}
161	SJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.
162	The SJ advantage over logprob remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\Delta\rho{\approx}0.08$--$0.20$), and three independent complexity proxies (entity count, sentence length, relation density) show weak mutual correlation ($|r|{<}0.15$), indicating a genuinely multi-dimensional benefit rather than a single confound.
163	
164	\paragraph{Method boundary: WNUT-17.}
165	WNUT-17 establishes where the approach breaks down.
166	SJ achieves $\rho{=}0.062$ ($p{=}0.106$, not statistically significant), with 54\% of instances at zero F1.
167	Log-probability shows a strong \textit{negative} correlation ($\rho{=}{-}0.411$): the model is confidently wrong on novel entities, a calibration failure that no post-hoc consistency signal can remedy.
168	This is a boundary of the method, not a limitation of the datasets.
169	
170	\begin{table}[t]
171	\centering
172	\small
173	\caption{Signal robustness across temperature and sample size on SciERC (Spearman $\rho$). $^\dagger$Mean over 3 seeds ($\sigma{=}0.025$; individual: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384).}
174	\label{tab:robustness}
175	\begin{tabular}{llcc}
176	\toprule
177	& & NER $\rho$ & RE $\rho$ \\
178	\midrule
179	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Temperature} ($N{=}8$)} \\
180	\quad SJ & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.388 & 0.234 \\
181	\quad SJ & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.357 & 0.211 \\
182	\quad FK & $T{=}0.7$ & 0.321 & 0.184 \\
183	\quad FK & $T{=}1.0$ & 0.265 & 0.253 \\
184	\midrule
185	\multicolumn{4}{l}{\textit{Sample size} ($T{=}1.0$)} \\
186	\quad SJ & $N{=}8$  & 0.357 & 0.211 \\
187	\quad SJ & $N{=}16$ & 0.412$^\dagger$ & 0.281 \\
188	\quad FK & $N{=}8$  & 0.265 & 0.253 \\
189	\quad FK & $N{=}16$ & 0.280 & 0.279 \\
190	\bottomrule
191	\end{tabular}
192	\end{table}
193	
194	\paragraph{Robustness analysis.}
195	Table~\ref{tab:robustness} reports signal stability across temperature and sample size on SciERC.
196	\textit{Temperature.}
197	SJ NER $\rho$ shifts from 0.388 ($T{=}0.7$) to 0.357 ($T{=}1.0$), a difference of 0.031 whose bootstrap confidence interval includes zero.
198	FK shows mixed behavior: NER $\rho$ drops by 0.056, while RE $\rho$ increases by 0.069, likely reflecting reduced diversity at lower temperature interacting differently across tasks.
199	Across all comparisons, the SJ--FK relative ordering is preserved at both temperatures on NER (0.388 vs.\ 0.321 at $T{=}0.7$; 0.357 vs.\ 0.265 at $T{=}1.0$), indicating a stable signal hierarchy despite numerically small temperature effects.
200	
201	\textit{Sample size.}
202	Increasing from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ yields meaningful gains for SJ on NER: $\rho$ improves from 0.357 to $0.412{\pm}0.025$ (three seeds: 0.428, 0.424, 0.384).
203	FK benefits less ($\rho{\approx}0.280$ at $N{=}16$ vs.\ 0.265 at $N{=}8$).
204	For RE at $N{=}16$, SJ ($\rho{=}0.281$), FK ($\rho{=}0.279$), and logprob ($\rho{=}0.287$) all plateau at comparable levels, suggesting that compound entity-then-relation errors impose a ceiling that additional samples cannot overcome.
205	
206	\paragraph{Cross-model transfer.}
207	LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct replicates the structural-over-surface ordering on SciERC NER (SJ $\rho{=}0.325{\pm}0.021$, FK $\rho{=}0.256{\pm}0.024$, 3-seed mean).
208	On CoNLL, the cross-model gap is minimal (Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}): SJ achieves $\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA) vs.\ 0.436 (Qwen), $\Delta{=}0.005$.
209	On conditional RE (non-zero F1 instances), SJ recovers to $\rho{=}0.405$ while FK drops to $\rho{=}0.259$, confirming that structural advantage is robust across both models and evaluation conditions.
210	
211	\paragraph{Evaluation circularity.}
212	Leave-one-out SJ (LOO-SJ), which excludes the target sample when computing confidence, yields $\Delta\rho{=}{-}0.014$ relative to standard SJ, with a maximum per-instance $\Delta\rho$ of $3.1{\times}10^{-4}$.
213	Evaluation circularity is negligible.
214	
215	\paragraph{Conditional filtering reveals signal divergence.}
216	Filtering to non-zero F1 instances improves SJ correlations: NER $\rho{=}0.412$ (from 0.357), RE $\rho{=}0.368$ at $N{=}8$.
217	At $N{=}16$, signal families diverge sharply on conditional RE: SJ maintains $\rho{=}0.308$, while logprob drops to $\rho{=}{-}0.026$---effectively zero---showing that logprob's apparent RE utility at $N{=}8$ does not generalize to larger sample sets.
218	For applications that can first filter out catastrophic failures, SJ's effective ranking approaches $\rho{\approx}0.4$ on both NER and RE, a regime where quality-aware aggregation can provide meaningful benefit.
219	
220	\paragraph{Binary filtering vs.\ continuous ranking.}
221	The logprob paradox observed in Table~\ref{tab:quality_estimation}---highest AUROC (0.761) but lowest $\rho$ (0.307) on CoNLL with LLaMA---reflects a fundamental distinction in how signals encode quality information.
222	Log-probability provides coarse separation between reliable and unreliable instances (suited for accept/reject thresholds), while structural signals like SJ and Voting provide finer-grained ranking (suited for prioritization and quality-aware aggregation).
223	Figure~\ref{fig:reliability} visualizes this complementarity across the difficulty spectrum.
224	Practitioners should select signal type based on the downstream application: binary filtering favors logprob; ranking-dependent decisions favor structural consistency.
225	
```

      "content": "\\section{Experiments}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:setup}\n\n\\paragraph{Datasets.}\nWe evaluate on three English IE benchmarks spanning a difficulty gradient.\n\\textbf{SciERC} \\cite{luan2018scierc} contains 551 test instances from scientific abstracts annotated for joint NER (6 entity types) and RE (7 relation types), representing a complex schema with overlapping structures.\n\\textbf{CoNLL-2003} \\cite{tjongkimsang2003conll} provides 3{,}453 test sentences from newswire with 4 entity types, serving as a high-accuracy benchmark where models achieve ${>}$91\\% micro F1.\n\\textbf{WNUT-17} \\cite{derczynski2017wnut} consists of 1{,}287 test instances from social media with emerging entities, used to probe method boundaries where model capability is insufficient.\nThis gradient---from near-solved (CoNLL) through challenging (SciERC) to infeasible (WNUT-17)---enables systematic characterization of how signal effectiveness varies with task difficulty (\\S\\ref{sec:complexity_robustness}).\n\n\\paragraph{Models.}\nOur primary model is \\textbf{Qwen3-8B}, fine-tuned with LoRA ($r{=}32$) separately per dataset.\nFor cross-model validation, we evaluate \\textbf{LLaMA-3.1-8B-Instruct} to assess whether structural confidence signals transfer across architectures and training regimes.\n\n\\paragraph{Sampling and Evaluation.}\nFor each test instance, we draw $N$ independent samples at temperature $T{=}1.0$, with $T{=}0.7$ for robustness analysis.\nWe use $N{=}8$ for both SciERC and CoNLL in the primary evaluation, and $N{=}16$ for SciERC scaling experiments.\nQuality estimation is measured along two axes: \\textit{rank correlation} with instance-level F1 via Spearman $\\rho$ and Kendall $\\tau$, and \\textit{binary discrimination} via AUROC (instances above vs.\\ below median F1).\nDownstream utility is assessed through \\textbf{best-of-$N$ selection F1}: the F1 obtained when each signal selects one sample per instance.\nInstance-level F1 uses exact span matching for NER and head-tail-label matching for RE, where a relation is correct only when both argument entities and the relation label match the gold annotation.\nThe greedy decoding baseline ($T{=}0$, $N{=}1$) establishes performance without test-time scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Confidence Signals.}\nWe compare five signals from three families (detailed in \\S\\ref{sec:signals}).\n\\textit{Structural} signals operate on extracted entity/relation sets: \\textbf{Voting Confidence} aggregates per-extraction frequency across samples, and \\textbf{Soft Jaccard (SJ)} measures pairwise set similarity \\cite{jaccard1901distribution}.\n\\textit{Surface} signals operate on string representations: \\textbf{Fleiss' $\\kappa$ (FK)} quantifies inter-sample agreement treating samples as independent annotators \\cite{fleiss1971agreement}, and \\textbf{Exact Match (EM)} computes the fraction of identical sample pairs.\nThe \\textit{model-internal} signal is \\textbf{log-probability (logprob)}, the mean token-level log-likelihood.\n\n\\paragraph{Implementation.}\nInference uses vLLM with XGrammar constrained decoding \\cite{willard2023guided} to enforce valid JSON output structure.\nA span realignment post-processing step corrects character-level offset mismatches between generated and gold annotation spans.\nAll confidence signals are computed from the $N$ samples without access to gold labels.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Quality Estimation Results}\n\\label{sec:qe_results}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Quality estimation across tasks and models (Spearman $\\rho$ and AUROC). All results use $N{=}8$ samples at $T{=}1.0$. Best value per column in \\textbf{bold}. SciERC evaluates joint NER (6 types) and RE (7 types); CoNLL evaluates NER (4 types) with two model families.}\n\\label{tab:quality_estimation}\n\\begin{tabular}{ll cc cc cc cc}\n\\toprule\n& & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC NER}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{SciERC RE}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (Qwen)}} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textbf{CoNLL (LLaMA)}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){3-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6} \\cmidrule(lr){7-8} \\cmidrule(lr){9-10}\nSignal & Family & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC & $\\rho$ & AUC \\\\\n\\midrule\nVoting Conf. & Structural & \\textbf{.502} & \\textbf{.728} & \\textbf{.422} & \\textbf{.716} & .424 & .789 & .426 & .709 \\\\\nSoft Jaccard  & Structural & .357 & .666 & .211 & .582 & .436 & .801 & \\textbf{.431} & .704 \\\\\nFleiss' $\\kappa$ & Surface & .265 & .624 & .253 & .631 & .400 & .774 & .428 & .703 \\\\\nExact Match  & Surface    & .257 & .623 & .100 & .504 & \\textbf{.451} & \\textbf{.816} & \\textbf{.431} & .705 \\\\\nLog-prob     & Model      & .140 & .559 & .247 & .635 & .316 & .753 & .307 & \\textbf{.761} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:quality_estimation} presents rank correlation ($\\rho$) and AUROC for five confidence signals across tasks and models.\n\n\\paragraph{Structural signals dominate on complex schemas.}\nOn SciERC NER, Voting Confidence achieves $\\rho{=}0.502$, substantially outperforming the second-best signal, SJ ($\\rho{=}0.357$), and all remaining signals ($\\rho{\\leq}0.265$).\nVoting's advantage reflects its extraction-level granularity: it rewards entities that appear consistently while penalizing hallucinated or missed ones, whereas SJ compares entire sets and is sensitive to both overlap and total extraction count.\nThe structural advantage persists on RE, where Voting ($\\rho{=}0.422$) again leads.\nHowever, all signals weaken on RE: 44\\% of instances receive zero F1, creating a floor effect that compresses the discriminable range.\nNotably, FK ($\\rho{=}0.253$) exceeds SJ ($\\rho{=}0.211$) on RE---a reversal from NER that shows no single structural signal dominates across both tasks.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal rankings shift with task difficulty.}\nOn CoNLL NER, where greedy decoding achieves ${>}$91\\% micro F1, the signal landscape changes substantially.\nExact Match leads with $\\rho{=}0.451$ and AUROC${=}0.816$, surpassing SJ ($\\rho{=}0.436$) and Voting ($\\rho{=}0.424$).\nWhen most samples are near-correct, binary agreement captures the remaining variance more efficiently than set-level similarity.\nThis reversal---EM${>}$SJ on easy tasks vs.\\ SJ${>}$EM on hard tasks---demonstrates that optimal signal choice depends on intrinsic task difficulty.\n\n\\paragraph{Cross-model stability.}\nComparing Qwen and LLaMA on CoNLL, SJ shows remarkable stability: $\\rho{=}0.436$ (Qwen) vs.\\ $\\rho{=}0.431$ (LLaMA), a gap of only 0.005.\nThis model-agnostic property is expected for structural signals that operate on outputs rather than internal model states, and provides the strongest evidence that SJ generalizes across architectures.\nThe SJ--FK gap also narrows with schema simplification: $\\Delta\\rho{=}0.092$ on SciERC NER (6 entity types) vs.\\ $\\Delta\\rho{=}0.003$ on CoNLL with LLaMA (4 types), suggesting that set-level structure contributes less when the type inventory is small.\n\n\\paragraph{The logprob paradox.}\nLog-probability reveals a dissociation between ranking and classification.\nOn CoNLL with LLaMA, it achieves the highest AUROC (0.761) yet the lowest $\\rho$ (0.307) among all signals.\nThis pattern indicates that logprob assigns well-separated scores to easy vs.\\ hard instances (strong binary discrimination) but provides little resolution within each group (weak ranking), making it better suited for binary filtering than continuous quality estimation.\nWe return to this distinction in \\S\\ref{sec:complexity_robustness} and visualize it in Figure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap}.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle headroom and practical ceilings.}\nOn CoNLL, oracle best-of-$N$ selection yields only +4.2~pp (Qwen) and +1.8~pp (LLaMA) over greedy.\nWhen the model already operates near its performance ceiling, confidence signals serve as \\textit{reliability assurance}---confirming that outputs are trustworthy---rather than enabling selective prediction gains.\nWe therefore focus selection analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:selection_gap}) on SciERC, where oracle headroom exceeds 16~pp.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{The Correlation-Selection Gap}\n\\label{sec:selection_gap}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Best-of-$N$ selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$, $T{=}1.0$). Best non-oracle result per column in \\textbf{bold}. EM produces binary agreement without sample ranking.}\n\\label{tab:selection}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & NER F1 & RE F1 \\\\\n\\midrule\nOracle & 0.769 & 0.561 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLog-prob           & 0.609 & \\textbf{0.397} \\\\\nEnsemble (SJ+Vot.) & 0.604 & 0.367 \\\\\nSoft Jaccard       & 0.604 & 0.368 \\\\\nVoting             & 0.596 & 0.338 \\\\\nExact Match        & ---   & ---   \\\\\n\\midrule\nGreedy ($T{=}0$)   & \\textbf{0.609} & 0.374 \\\\\nRandom             & 0.580 & 0.338 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nDespite strong quality estimation, confidence signals yield limited gains when used for best-of-$N$ selection.\nTable~\\ref{tab:selection} compares selection F1 on SciERC ($N{=}8$).\n\n\\paragraph{NER: no signal surpasses greedy.}\nThe oracle upper bound (0.769) reveals 16.0~pp of headroom above greedy (0.609).\nYet no confidence signal closes this gap.\nLog-probability (0.609) merely ties greedy; the best quality estimator, Voting ($\\rho{=}0.502$), selects to only 0.596, \\textit{below} greedy.\nAn ensemble of SJ and Voting (AUROC${=}0.735$) also fails, achieving 0.604.\nThe greedy--logprob tie is revealing: log-probability selects the sample with highest model confidence, which on average matches the deterministic greedy output, implying that the $N$ samples cluster at a similar quality level with insufficient variance for any signal to exploit.\nThe Random baseline (0.580) confirms that not all samples are equal---uniform selection loses 2.9~pp vs.\\ greedy---but the discriminative structure captured by confidence signals adds no value beyond greedy's deterministic choice.\n\n\\paragraph{RE: logprob uniquely succeeds.}\nFor RE, log-probability is the only signal to exceed greedy (0.397 vs.\\ 0.374, +2.3~pp).\nStructural signals trail or match greedy: SJ selects to 0.368 and Voting to 0.338.\nThe oracle gap of 18.7~pp (0.561 vs.\\ 0.374) indicates substantially more per-instance variance than NER.\nLog-probability's success here may stem from its per-token nature: unlike instance-level consistency signals, logprob evaluates each sample independently, enabling it to distinguish a particularly successful extraction from its peers even when all extractions share similar structural consistency.\n\n\\paragraph{Quality estimation $\\neq$ output selection.}\nWe attribute this gap (Figure~\\ref{fig:selection_gap}) to a fundamental distinction: instance-level confidence signals predict \\textit{which inputs} the model handles reliably, not \\textit{which output} among $N$ candidates is best for a given input.\nWhen per-sample variance is low relative to instance-level difficulty---as in NER, where the model often produces $N$ similar extractions---even perfect quality estimation cannot discriminate among near-identical candidates.\n\n\\paragraph{Implications for test-time scaling.}\nThis correlation-selection gap has direct consequences for test-time scaling in IE \\cite{brown2024monkeys, snell2024scaling}.\nScaling inference compute through repeated sampling assumes a selector can identify the best sample.\nOur results show that for structured prediction, existing confidence signals serve as quality \\textit{estimators}---useful for flagging unreliable predictions---but not as effective \\textit{selectors}.\nClosing this gap likely requires signals that capture per-sample rather than per-instance variation, such as trained verifiers \\cite{cobbe2021verifiers} or pairwise comparison models.\n\n%% ============================================================\n\\subsection{Task Complexity and Signal Robustness}\n\\label{sec:complexity_robustness}\n\n\\paragraph{Task difficulty as a boundary condition.}\nSignal effectiveness varies systematically with task difficulty.\nOn CoNLL (greedy micro F1${>}$0.91), EM leads all signals ($\\rho{=}0.451$); on SciERC NER (micro F1$\\,{\\approx}\\,$0.66), SJ overtakes EM ($\\rho{=}0.357$ vs.\\ 0.257); on SciERC RE, where 44\\% of instances score zero F1, all signals converge to $\\rho{\\approx}0.25$.\nThe NER--RE difference is statistically confirmed: Fisher's $z$-test on $\\rho_\\text{SJ}$ yields $z{=}2.31$, $p{=}0.021$.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-relation diagnostic.}\nRE signal degradation is relation-specific.\n\\textsc{Feature-Of} has near-zero correlation ($\\rho{=}0.004$), while \\textsc{Hyponym-Of} achieves $\\rho{=}0.646$.\nThe primary bottleneck is entity span errors, which account for 55.5\\% of RE false negatives---a prerequisite failure that consistency signals cannot detect, as they measure agreement among equally incorrect extractions.\n\n\\paragraph{Capability stratification.}\nSJ effectiveness scales with structural complexity: on SciERC instances with 6+ entities, $\\rho{=}0.547$, compared to $\\rho{=}0.415$ for 1--2 entities.\nThe SJ advantage over logprob remains stable across sentence-length quartiles ($\\Delta\\rho{\\approx}0.08$--$0.20$), and three independent complexity proxies (entity count, sentence length, relation density) show weak mutual correlation ($|r|{<}0.15$), indicating a genuinely multi-dimensional benefit rather than a single confound.\n\n\\paragraph{Method boundary: WNUT-17.}\nWNUT-17 establishes where the approach breaks down.\nSJ achieves $\\rho{=}0.062$ ($p{=}0.106$, not statistically significant), with 54\\% of instances at zero F1.\nLog-probability shows a strong \\textit{negative} correlation ($\\rho{=}{-}0.411$): the model is confidently wrong on novel entities, a calibration failure that no post-hoc consistency signal can remedy.\nThis is a boundary of the method, not a limitation of the datasets.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Signal robustness across temperature and sample size on SciERC (Spearman $\\rho$). $^\\dagger$Mean over 3 seeds ($\\sigma{=}0.025$; hierarchy despite numerically small temperature effects.\n\n\\textit{Sample size.}\nIncreasing from $N{=}8$ to $N{=}16$ yields meaningful gains for SJ on NER: $\\rho$ improves from 0.357 to But EM and LP are far more sensitive to the max_tokens parameter — EM shifts by 0.038 and LP by 0.065 between 512 and 1024 tokens, with shorter sequences compressing logprob scores more severely. This means the Table 1 results in the paper are pulling from exp_012's 512-token configuration, where the voting confidence metric is inflated due to a known bug. Looking at the core issue: the paper's Table 1 contains inflated VC values (0.502 instead of the correct 0.379) because it pulls from exp_012 with max_tokens=512, while Table 4 correctly uses the rerun with max_tokens=1024 but falsely claims alignment with Table 1. This is particularly problematic since the paper's main narrative hinges on highlighting that inflated VC number as a key finding. The worker's analysis (D100) identified the cross-table mismatch but incorrectly pinpointed Table 1's source, leaving the underlying data integrity problem unresolved. The discrepancy between Table 1 (.357) and Table 4 (.360) persists even after the proposed fix, and I'm noticing several other issues D100 missed—the VC inflation at .502 and whether the inline text references need updating too. Now I'm digging into where Table 1's RE values actually come from, since they don't align with exp_012_rerun_1024 or the values listed in paper_framing.md for the full RE dataset. The memory explicitly flags VC=.502 as a bug that was corrected to .379, and the Paper Agent's workflow shows a data correction chain that should have fixed these values — so if the current paper is using the old numbers, that's a regression.
